========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hello. Well, I'm here because I've realized I keep arranging my own house around not seeing spiders. 

It sounds a bit ridiculous to say out loud, but... I put off doing laundry just because it's down in the basement. I avoid the garden shed entirely. I've even started paying people to do small jobs around the house that I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I simply can’t make myself go into those spaces. It's really starting to get in the way of my daily life, and I just need to get a handle on it.

I put off doing laundry just because it's down in the basement. I avoid the garden shed entirely. I've even started paying people to do small jobs around the house that I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I simply can’t make myself go into those spaces. It's really starting to get in the way of my daily life, and I just need to get a handle on it.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you see a spider or think about seeing one?\n\nPatient: Well, if I actually spot one, the reaction is pretty much instant. My chest tightens up right away, and my hands start shaking. I either just completely freeze, like right in the doorway, or I back out of the space so fast... it's really like my body has decided to get out of there before my brain can even think about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or activities, besides the ones related to spiders, that you avoid?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just the spiders. I don't have any issues with heights or enclosed spaces or flying or anything like that. I can go about my day normally, go to work at the school, run errands, socialize... as long as I don't have to deal with spiders. It's very specific to them.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of spiders is extreme?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it is. Mostly because... well, logically, I know a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I'm a sensible person, but the moment I picture one dropping out of a box, or just sitting there near the washer, it feels enormous and threatening in a way I’m honestly embarrassed to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack when you saw a spider or thought about seeing one?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've ever had a full-blown panic attack, no. I mean, my chest gets tight and I shake, like I mentioned, and my heart definitely races, but I've never felt like I was having a heart attack or couldn't breathe or anything like that. It's more just... a really strong, immediate urge to get away. I haven't ever had a panic attack just from thinking about them, either. It's mostly when I actually see one that the physical reaction hits.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social activities, no. I still see my friends and go out. But it has definitely affected my daily life at home. Owning this house has actually become more expensive and just... harder to manage. I end up paying for small maintenance jobs that I could probably do myself, just because they involve going into the basement or the shed. And I let chores back up, like laundry, if I've seen a spider down there. I'm basically avoiding parts of my own property that I need to use, which is frustrating.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It's more just... a really strong, immediate urge to get away. I haven't ever had a panic attack just from thinking about them, either. It's mostly when I actually see one that the physical reaction hits.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social activities, no. I still see my friends and go out. But it has definitely affected my daily life at home. Owning this house has actually become more expensive and just... harder to manage. I end up paying for small maintenance jobs that I could probably do myself, just because they involve going into the basement or the shed. And I let chores back up, like laundry, if I've seen a spider down there. I'm basically avoiding parts of my own property that I need to use, which is frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders ever affected your work at the school?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't really affected my work. I teach in a fairly modern, well-kept building, so spiders aren't something I run into there. If I had to go into a dusty supply closet or something, maybe it would be an issue, but so far, work has been completely fine. It's really just my house where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How would you rate the distress you feel when you think about or encounter spiders, on a scale from 1 to 10?\n\nPatient: I'd say... when I actually see one, it's probably around a 7 or an 8. It's really intense right in that moment. But just thinking about them? Maybe a 3 or a 4. It makes me uncomfortable, but it's manageable. It's the actual encounter that really spikes my distress.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my friends and family know I don't like them, but it hasn't caused any real issues or arguments. Like I said, I still go out and socialize just fine. It's really just a personal frustration at home, it doesn't spill over into my relationships.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello. Well, I'm here because I've realized I keep arranging my own house around not seeing spiders. \n\nIt sounds a bit ridiculous to say out loud, but... I put off doing laundry just because it's down in the basement. I avoid the garden shed entirely. I've even started paying people to do small jobs around the house that I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I simply can’t make myself go into those spaces. It's really starting to get in the way of my daily life, and I just need to get a handle on it.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you see a spider or think about seeing one?\n\nPatient: Well, if I actually spot one, the reaction is pretty much instant. My chest tightens up right away, and my hands start shaking. I either just completely freeze, like right in the doorway, or I back out of the space so fast... it's really like my body has decided to get out of there before my brain can even think about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or activities, besides the ones related to spiders, that you avoid?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just the spiders. I don't have any issues with heights or enclosed spaces or flying or anything like that. I can go about my day normally, go to work at the school, run errands, socialize... as long as I don't have to deal with spiders. It's very specific to them.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of spiders is extreme?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it is. Mostly because... well, logically, I know a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I'm a sensible person, but the moment I picture one dropping out of a box, or just sitting there near the washer, it feels enormous and threatening in a way I’m honestly embarrassed to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack when you saw a spider or thought about seeing one?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've ever had a full-blown panic attack, no. I mean, my chest gets tight and I shake, like I mentioned, and my heart definitely races, but I've never felt like I was having a heart attack or couldn't breathe or anything like that. It's more just... a really strong, immediate urge to get away. I haven't ever had a panic attack just from thinking about them, either. It's mostly when I actually see one that the physical reaction hits.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social activities, no. I still see my friends and go out. But it has definitely affected my daily life at home. Owning this house has actually become more expensive and just... harder to manage. I end up paying for small maintenance jobs that I could probably do myself, just because they involve going into the basement or the shed. And I let chores back up, like laundry, if I've seen a spider down there. I'm basically avoiding parts of my own property that I need to use, which is frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders ever affected your work at the school?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't really affected my work. I teach in a fairly modern, well-kept building, so spiders aren't something I run into there. If I had to go into a dusty supply closet or something, maybe it would be an issue, but so far, work has been completely fine. It's really just my house where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How would you rate the distress you feel when you think about or encounter spiders, on a scale from 1 to 10?\n\nPatient: I'd say... when I actually see one, it's probably around a 7 or an 8. It's really intense right in that moment. But just thinking about them? Maybe a 3 or a 4. It makes me uncomfortable, but it's manageable. It's the actual encounter that really spikes my distress.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my friends and family know I don't like them, but it hasn't caused any real issues or arguments. Like I said, I still go out and socialize just fine. It's really just a personal frustration at home, it doesn't spill over into my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily routines or tasks that you have to do differently because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Owning this house has actually gotten more expensive and harder to manage because of it. I've had to start paying for people to come do small maintenance jobs that I could easily do myself, just because they're in areas where I know there might be spiders. And sometimes I just let chores back up... like cleaning out the garage or the shed. I completely avoid certain parts of the property that I really need to use, just because I'm too afraid of running into one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would encounter spiders more often if you didn't avoid certain areas or tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I would. It's really just a personal frustration at home, it doesn't spill over into my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily routines or tasks that you have to do differently because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Owning this house has actually gotten more expensive and harder to manage because of it. I've had to start paying for people to come do small maintenance jobs that I could easily do myself, just because they're in areas where I know there might be spiders. And sometimes I just let chores back up... like cleaning out the garage or the shed. I completely avoid certain parts of the property that I really need to use, just because I'm too afraid of running into one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would encounter spiders more often if you didn't avoid certain areas or tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I would. I know they're out there, and staying away is the only way I can keep from running into them.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of spiders, or are you also afraid of other insects or small animals?\n\nPatient: It's really just spiders. I'm basically avoiding parts of my own property that I need to use, which is frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders ever affected your work at the school?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't really affected my work. I teach in a fairly modern, well-kept building, so spiders aren't something I run into there. If I had to go into a dusty supply closet or something, maybe it would be an issue, but so far, work has been completely fine. It's really just my house where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How would you rate the distress you feel when you think about or encounter spiders, on a scale from 1 to 10?\n\nPatient: I'd say... when I actually see one, it's probably around a 7 or an 8. It's really intense right in that moment. But just thinking about them? Maybe a 3 or a 4. It makes me uncomfortable, but it's manageable. It's the actual encounter that really spikes my distress.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my friends and family know I don't like them, but it hasn't caused any real issues or arguments. Like I said, I still go out and socialize just fine. It's really just a personal frustration at home, it doesn't spill over into my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily routines or tasks that you have to do differently because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Owning this house has actually gotten more expensive and harder to manage because of it. I've had to start paying for people to come do small maintenance jobs that I could easily do myself, just because they're in areas where I know there might be spiders. And sometimes I just let chores back up... like cleaning out the garage or the shed. I completely avoid certain parts of the property that I really need to use, just because I'm too afraid of running into one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would encounter spiders more often if you didn't avoid certain areas or tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I would. If I were actually going into the shed or the crawlspace, or doing the yard work around the bushes myself, I'm sure I'd see more of them. That's exactly why I avoid those areas. I know they're out there, and staying away is the only way I can keep from running into them.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of spiders, or are you also afraid of other insects or small animals?\n\nPatient: It's really just spiders. I don't have a problem with ants or beetles or mice or anything like that. I mean, logically, I know a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But... the moment I even picture one dropping out of a box in the garage, or sitting near the washer when I'm doing laundry, it just feels enormous and threatening. It's a reaction that feels completely out of my control, in a way I’m honestly pretty embarrassed to admit. But yeah, it's just spiders.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders caused you any significant problems at work or in your social life, beyond just the issues with maintaining your house?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It doesn't affect my work at all. I teach high school, and the school is kept pretty clean, so I rarely have to worry about them there. And socially, it's not an issue either. My friends know I don't like spiders, but it doesn't stop me from going out or visiting them or anything like that. The problems are really just isolated to my house and the yard.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello. Well, I'm here because I've realized I keep arranging my own house around not seeing spiders. \n\nIt sounds a bit ridiculous to say out loud, but... I put off doing laundry just because it's down in the basement. I avoid the garden shed entirely. I've even started paying people to do small jobs around the house that I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I simply can’t make myself go into those spaces. It's really starting to get in the way of my daily life, and I just need to get a handle on it.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you see a spider or think about seeing one?\n\nPatient: Well, if I actually spot one, the reaction is pretty much instant. My chest tightens up right away, and my hands start shaking. I either just completely freeze, like right in the doorway, or I back out of the space so fast... it's really like my body has decided to get out of there before my brain can even think about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or activities, besides the ones related to spiders, that you avoid?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just the spiders. I don't have any issues with heights or enclosed spaces or flying or anything like that. I can go about my day normally, go to work at the school, run errands, socialize... as long as I don't have to deal with spiders. It's very specific to them.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of spiders is extreme?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it is. Mostly because... well, logically, I know a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I'm a sensible person, but the moment I picture one dropping out of a box, or just sitting there near the washer, it feels enormous and threatening in a way I’m honestly embarrassed to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack when you saw a spider or thought about seeing one?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've ever had a full-blown panic attack, no. I mean, my chest gets tight and I shake, like I mentioned, and my heart definitely races, but I've never felt like I was having a heart attack or couldn't breathe or anything like that. It's more just... a really strong, immediate urge to get away. I haven't ever had a panic attack just from thinking about them, either. It's mostly when I actually see one that the physical reaction hits.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social activities, no. I still see my friends and go out. But it has definitely affected my daily life at home. Owning this house has actually become more expensive and just... harder to manage. I end up paying for small maintenance jobs that I could probably do myself, just because they involve going into the basement or the shed. And I let chores back up, like laundry, if I've seen a spider down there. I'm basically avoiding parts of my own property that I need to use, which is frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders ever affected your work at the school?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't really affected my work. I teach in a fairly modern, well-kept building, so spiders aren't something I run into there. If I had to go into a dusty supply closet or something, maybe it would be an issue, but so far, work has been completely fine. It's really just my house where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How would you rate the distress you feel when you think about or encounter spiders, on a scale from 1 to 10?\n\nPatient: I'd say... when I actually see one, it's probably around a 7 or an 8. It's really intense right in that moment. But just thinking about them? Maybe a 3 or a 4. It makes me uncomfortable, but it's manageable. It's the actual encounter that really spikes my distress.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my friends and family know I don't like them, but it hasn't caused any real issues or arguments. Like I said, I still go out and socialize just fine. It's really just a personal frustration at home, it doesn't spill over into my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily routines or tasks that you have to do differently because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Owning this house has actually gotten more expensive and harder to manage because of it. I've had to start paying for people to come do small maintenance jobs that I could easily do myself, just because they're in areas where I know there might be spiders. And sometimes I just let chores back up... like cleaning out the garage or the shed. I completely avoid certain parts of the property that I really need to use, just because I'm too afraid of running into one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would encounter spiders more often if you didn't avoid certain areas or tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I would. If I were actually going into the shed or the crawlspace, or doing the yard work around the bushes myself, I'm sure I'd see more of them. That's exactly why I avoid those areas. I know they're out there, and staying away is the only way I can keep from running into them.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of spiders, or are you also afraid of other insects or small animals?\n\nPatient: It's really just spiders. I don't have a problem with ants or beetles or mice or anything like that. I mean, logically, I know a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But... the moment I even picture one dropping out of a box in the garage, or sitting near the washer when I'm doing laundry, it just feels enormous and threatening. It's a reaction that feels completely out of my control, in a way I’m honestly pretty embarrassed to admit. But yeah, it's just spiders.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders caused you any significant problems at work or in your social life, beyond just the issues with maintaining your house?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It doesn't affect my work at all. I teach high school, and the school is kept pretty clean, so I rarely have to worry about them there. And socially, it's not an issue either. My friends know I don't like spiders, but it doesn't stop me from going out or visiting them or anything like that. The problems are really just isolated to my house and the yard.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of spiders was interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, mostly just around my home. I mean, owning this house has actually become more expensive and just... harder to manage because of it. I end up paying people for small maintenance help—things I could easily do myself—just so I don't have to go into the shed or the crawlspace. And I let certain chores back up because I'm putting off going into the basement or the garage. So I'm basically avoiding parts of my own property that I really need to use. It definitely makes my daily life more difficult than it should be.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been afraid of spiders?\n\nPatient: It's been about thirteen months now. It started shortly after I moved into this house... I was unpacking boxes and found some spiders, and it's just been an issue ever since then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It's really starting to get in the way of my daily life, and I just need to get a handle on it.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you see a spider or think about seeing one?\n\nPatient: Well, if I actually spot one, the reaction is pretty much instant. My chest tightens up right away, and my hands start shaking. I either just completely freeze, like right in the doorway, or I back out of the space so fast... it's really like my body has decided to get out of there before my brain can even think about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or activities, besides the ones related to spiders, that you avoid?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just the spiders. I don't have any issues with heights or enclosed spaces or flying or anything like that. It's very specific to them.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of spiders is extreme?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it is. Mostly because... well, logically, I know a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I'm a sensible person, but the moment I picture one dropping out of a box, or just sitting there near the washer, it feels enormous and threatening in a way I’m honestly embarrassed to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack when you saw a spider or thought about seeing one?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've ever had a full-blown panic attack, no. I mean, my chest gets tight and I shake, like I mentioned, and my heart definitely races, but I've never felt like I was having a heart attack or couldn't breathe or anything like that. It's more just... a really strong, immediate urge to get away. I haven't ever had a panic attack just from thinking about them, either. It's mostly when I actually see one that the physical reaction hits.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social activities, no. I still see my friends and go out. But it has definitely affected my daily life at home. Owning this house has actually become more expensive and just... harder to manage. I end up paying for small maintenance jobs that I could probably do myself, just because they involve going into the basement or the shed. And I let chores back up, like laundry, if I've seen a spider down there. I'm basically avoiding parts of my own property that I need to use, which is frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders ever affected your work at the school?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't really affected my work. I teach in a fairly modern, well-kept building, so spiders aren't something I run into there. It's really just my house where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How would you rate the distress you feel when you think about or encounter spiders, on a scale from 1 to 10?\n\nPatient: I'd say... when I actually see one, it's probably around a 7 or an 8. It's really intense right in that moment. But just thinking about them? Maybe a 3 or a 4. It makes me uncomfortable, but it's manageable. It's the actual encounter that really spikes my distress.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my friends and family know I don't like them, but it hasn't caused any real issues or arguments. Like I said, I still go out and socialize just fine. It's really just a personal frustration at home, it doesn't spill over into my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily routines or tasks that you have to do differently because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Owning this house has actually gotten more expensive and harder to manage because of it. It's just the spiders. I don't have any issues with heights or enclosed spaces or flying or anything like that. I'm a sensible person, but the moment I picture one dropping out of a box, or just sitting there near the washer, it feels enormous and threatening in a way I’m honestly embarrassed to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack when you saw a spider or thought about seeing one?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've ever had a full-blown panic attack, no. I mean, my chest gets tight and I shake, like I mentioned, and my heart definitely races, but I've never felt like I was having a heart attack or couldn't breathe or anything like that. It's more just... a really strong, immediate urge to get away. I haven't ever had a panic attack just from thinking about them, either. It's mostly when I actually see one that the physical reaction hits.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social activities, no. I still see my friends and go out. But it has definitely affected my daily life at home. Owning this house has actually become more expensive and just... harder to manage. I end up paying for small maintenance jobs that I could probably do myself, just because they involve going into the basement or the shed. And I let chores back up, like laundry, if I've seen a spider down there. I'm basically avoiding parts of my own property that I need to use, which is frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders ever affected your work at the school?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't really affected my work. I teach in a fairly modern, well-kept building, so spiders aren't something I run into there. It's really just my house where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How would you rate the distress you feel when you think about or encounter spiders, on a scale from 1 to 10?\n\nPatient: I'd say... when I actually see one, it's probably around a 7 or an 8. It's the actual encounter that really spikes my distress.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's really just a personal frustration at home, it doesn't spill over into my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily routines or tasks that you have to do differently because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Owning this house has actually gotten more expensive and harder to manage because of it. I've had to start paying for people to come do small maintenance jobs that I could easily do myself, just because they're in areas where I know there might be spiders. And sometimes I just let chores back up... like cleaning out the garage or the shed. I completely avoid certain parts of the property that I really need to use, just because I'm too afraid of running into one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would encounter spiders more often if you didn't avoid certain areas or tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I would. It's really starting to get in the way of my daily life, and I just need to get a handle on it.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you see a spider or think about seeing one?\n\nPatient: Well, if I actually spot one, the reaction is pretty much instant. My chest tightens up right away, and my hands start shaking. I either just completely freeze, like right in the doorway, or I back out of the space so fast... it's really like my body has decided to get out of there before my brain can even think about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or activities, besides the ones related to spiders, that you avoid?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just the spiders. I don't have any issues with heights or enclosed spaces or flying or anything like that. I can go about my day normally, go to work at the school, run errands, socialize... as long as I don't have to deal with spiders. It's very specific to them.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of spiders is extreme?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it is. Mostly because... well, logically, I know a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I'm a sensible person, but the moment I picture one dropping out of a box, or just sitting there near the washer, it feels enormous and threatening in a way I’m honestly embarrassed to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack when you saw a spider or thought about seeing one?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've ever had a full-blown panic attack, no. I mean, my chest gets tight and I shake, like I mentioned, and my heart definitely races, but I've never felt like I was having a heart attack or couldn't breathe or anything like that. It's more just... a really strong, immediate urge to get away. I haven't ever had a panic attack just from thinking about them, either. It's mostly when I actually see one that the physical reaction hits.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social activities, no. I still see my friends and go out. But it has definitely affected my daily life at home. Owning this house has actually become more expensive and just... harder to manage. I end up paying for small maintenance jobs that I could probably do myself, just because they involve going into the basement or the shed. And I let chores back up, like laundry, if I've seen a spider down there. I'm basically avoiding parts of my own property that I need to use, which is frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders ever affected your work at the school?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't really affected my work. I teach in a fairly modern, well-kept building, so spiders aren't something I run into there. If I had to go into a dusty supply closet or something, maybe it would be an issue, but so far, work has been completely fine. It's really just my house where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How would you rate the distress you feel when you think about or encounter spiders, on a scale from 1 to 10?\n\nPatient: I'd say... when I actually see one, it's probably around a 7 or an 8. It's really intense right in that moment. But just thinking about them? Maybe a 3 or a 4. It makes me uncomfortable, but it's manageable. It's the actual encounter that really spikes my distress.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my friends and family know I don't like them, but it hasn't caused any real issues or arguments. Like I said, I still go out and socialize just fine. It's really just a personal frustration at home, it doesn't spill over into my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily routines or tasks that you have to do differently because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Owning this house has actually gotten more expensive and harder to manage because of it. I've had to start paying for people to come do small maintenance jobs that I could easily do myself, just because they're in areas where I know there might be spiders. And sometimes I just let chores back up... like cleaning out the garage or the shed. I completely avoid certain parts of the property that I really need to use, just because I'm too afraid of running into one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would encounter spiders more often if you didn't avoid certain areas or tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I would. If I were actually going into the shed or the crawlspace, or doing the yard work around the bushes myself, I'm sure I'd see more of them. That's exactly why I avoid those areas. I know they're out there, and staying away is the only way I can keep from running into them.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of spiders, or are you also afraid of other insects or small animals?\n\nPatient: It's really just spiders. I don't have a problem with ants or beetles or mice or anything like that. I mean, logically, I know a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But... the moment I even picture one dropping out of a box in the garage, or sitting near the washer when I'm doing laundry, it just feels enormous and threatening. It's a reaction that feels completely out of my control, in a way I’m honestly pretty embarrassed to admit. But yeah, it's just spiders.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders caused you any significant problems at work or in your social life, beyond just the issues with maintaining your house?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It doesn't affect my work at all. I teach high school, and the school is kept pretty clean, so I rarely have to worry about them there. And socially, it's not an issue either. My friends know I don't like spiders, but it doesn't stop me from going out or visiting them or anything like that. The problems are really just isolated to my house and the yard.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of spiders was interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, mostly just around my home. I mean, owning this house has actually become more expensive and just... harder to manage because of it. I end up paying people for small maintenance help—things I could easily do myself—just so I don't have to go into the shed or the crawlspace. And I let certain chores back up because I'm putting off going into the basement or the garage. So I'm basically avoiding parts of my own property that I really need to use. It definitely makes my daily life more difficult than it should be.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been afraid of spiders?\n\nPatient: It's been about thirteen months now. It started shortly after I moved into this house... I was unpacking boxes and found some spiders, and it's just been an issue ever since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to gradually expose yourself to spiders or situations where you might encounter them, to try and overcome your fear?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really tried anything like that. I know some people do that sort of thing, but honestly, the thought of doing it on purpose just... I haven't wanted to try it. I just try to avoid them as much as I can.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack just from thinking about spiders, without actually seeing one?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I definitely don't like thinking about them, and it makes me uncomfortable, but it's never caused a full panic attack or anything like that. The real panic only happens if I actually see one or if I'm in a spot where I know one is right there. Just thinking about it doesn't do that to me.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of all types of spiders, or is your fear limited to specific kinds?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it's pretty much all of them. I don't really stop to look and see what kind it is. If it's a spider, I just want to get away from it. So, no, it's not limited to any specific type... just spiders in general.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I still see my friends and go out. But it has definitely affected my daily life at home. Owning this house has actually become more expensive and just... harder to manage. I end up paying for small maintenance jobs that I could probably do myself, just because they involve going into the basement or the shed. And I let chores back up, like laundry, if I've seen a spider down there. I'm basically avoiding parts of my own property that I need to use, which is frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders ever affected your work at the school?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't really affected my work. I teach in a fairly modern, well-kept building, so spiders aren't something I run into there. If I had to go into a dusty supply closet or something, maybe it would be an issue, but so far, work has been completely fine. It's really just my house where it's a problem.\n\nDoctor: How would you rate the distress you feel when you think about or encounter spiders, on a scale from 1 to 10?\n\nPatient: I'd say... when I actually see one, it's probably around a 7 or an 8. It's really intense right in that moment. But just thinking about them? Maybe a 3 or a 4. It makes me uncomfortable, but it's manageable. It's the actual encounter that really spikes my distress.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my friends and family know I don't like them, but it hasn't caused any real issues or arguments. Like I said, I still go out and socialize just fine. It's really just a personal frustration at home, it doesn't spill over into my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily routines or tasks that you have to do differently because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Owning this house has actually gotten more expensive and harder to manage because of it. I've had to start paying for people to come do small maintenance jobs that I could easily do myself, just because they're in areas where I know there might be spiders. And sometimes I just let chores back up... like cleaning out the garage or the shed. I completely avoid certain parts of the property that I really need to use, just because I'm too afraid of running into one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would encounter spiders more often if you didn't avoid certain areas or tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I would. If I were actually going into the shed or the crawlspace, or doing the yard work around the bushes myself, I'm sure I'd see more of them. That's exactly why I avoid those areas. I know they're out there, and staying away is the only way I can keep from running into them.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of spiders, or are you also afraid of other insects or small animals?\n\nPatient: It's really just spiders. I don't have a problem with ants or beetles or mice or anything like that. I mean, logically, I know a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But... the moment I even picture one dropping out of a box in the garage, or sitting near the washer when I'm doing laundry, it just feels enormous and threatening. It's a reaction that feels completely out of my control, in a way I’m honestly pretty embarrassed to admit. But yeah, it's just spiders.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders caused you any significant problems at work or in your social life, beyond just the issues with maintaining your house?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It doesn't affect my work at all. I teach high school, and the school is kept pretty clean, so I rarely have to worry about them there. And socially, it's not an issue either. My friends know I don't like spiders, but it doesn't stop me from going out or visiting them or anything like that. The problems are really just isolated to my house and the yard.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of spiders was interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, mostly just around my home. I mean, owning this house has actually become more expensive and just... harder to manage because of it. I end up paying people for small maintenance help—things I could easily do myself—just so I don't have to go into the shed or the crawlspace. And I let certain chores back up because I'm putting off going into the basement or the garage. So I'm basically avoiding parts of my own property that I really need to use. It definitely makes my daily life more difficult than it should be.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been afraid of spiders?\n\nPatient: It's been about thirteen months now. It started shortly after I moved into this house... I was unpacking boxes and found some spiders, and it's just been an issue ever since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to gradually expose yourself to spiders or situations where you might encounter them, to try and overcome your fear?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really tried anything like that. I know some people do that sort of thing, but honestly, the thought of doing it on purpose just... I haven't wanted to try it. I just try to avoid them as much as I can.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack just from thinking about spiders, without actually seeing one?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I definitely don't like thinking about them, and it makes me uncomfortable, but it's never caused a full panic attack or anything like that. The real panic only happens if I actually see one or if I'm in a spot where I know one is right there. Just thinking about it doesn't do that to me.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of all types of spiders, or is your fear limited to specific kinds?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it's pretty much all of them. I don't really stop to look and see what kind it is. If it's a spider, I just want to get away from it. So, no, it's not limited to any specific type... just spiders in general.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders more about their appearance or about the possibility of them biting or harming you?\n\nPatient: It's definitely more about their appearance and just... the idea of them. I mean, I know a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn't going to actually hurt me. I know that logically. But the moment I picture one dropping out of a storage box in the garage or sitting near the washer in the basement... it just feels enormous and threatening to me. It's not really about getting bitten, it's just their presence. It's a bit embarrassing to admit how much it gets to me, honestly, but that's just how it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
